using Xunit;
using Calc;

namespace Calc.Tests
{
    public class CalculatorTest
    {
        [Fact]
        public void TestAddTwoPositiveIntegersReturnsSum()
        {
            // If Add is called with two positive integers (e.g., 5 and 10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (15). This verifies the basic functionality described in the documentation for standard positive inputs.
            var result = Calculator.Add(5, 10);
            Assert.Equal(15, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddTwoNegativeIntegersReturnsSum()
        {
            // If Add is called with two negative integers (e.g., -5 and -10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (-15). This verifies that the addition logic correctly handles negative numbers as implied by 'Adds two integers'.
            var result = Calculator.Add(-5, -10);
            Assert.Equal(-15, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddPositiveAndNegativeIntegersReturnsSum()
        {
            // If Add is called with a positive integer and a negative integer (e.g., 10 and -5), then the method should return the correct net result (5). This tests the handling of mixed signs.
            var result = Calculator.Add(10, -5);
            Assert.Equal(5, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddOppositeNumbersReturnsZero()
        {
            // If Add is called with two integers that are additive inverses (e.g., 5 and -5), then the method should return 0. This verifies the identity property of addition for non-zero inputs.
            var result = Calculator.Add(5, -5);
            Assert.Equal(0, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddZeroToPositiveIntegerReturnsOriginal()
        {
            // If Add is called with a positive integer and zero (e.g., 10 and 0), then the method should return the original positive integer (10). This verifies that zero acts as the additive identity.
            var result = Calculator.Add(10, 0);
            Assert.Equal(10, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddZeroToNegativeIntegerReturnsOriginal()
        {
            // If Add is called with a negative integer and zero (e.g., -10 and 0), then the method should return the original negative integer (-10). This verifies that zero acts as the additive identity for negative numbers.
            var result = Calculator.Add(-10, 0);
            Assert.Equal(-10, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddZeroToZeroReturnsZero()
        {
            // If Add is called with zero and zero (0 and 0), then the method should return 0. This is the most basic case of the additive identity.
            var result = Calculator.Add(0, 0);
            Assert.Equal(0, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddMaxIntAndMinIntReturnsMinusOne()
        {
            // If Add is called with int.MaxValue and int.MinValue, then the method should return -1. This tests the boundary behavior where the sum does not overflow the 32-bit integer range but hits the lower boundary limit.
            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MaxValue, int.MinValue);
            Assert.Equal(-1, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddTwoMaxIntegersHandlesOverflow()
        {
            // If Add is called with int.MaxValue and int.MaxValue, then the method must handle the overflow condition consistent with C# default integer arithmetic (unchecked wrap-around to int.MinValue + 2, i.e., -2) or throw OverflowException if checked. Since documentation does not specify exception handling, we verify the actual implementation behavior against the default CLR behavior for 'int' addition, which is wrap-around. Thus, it should return -2.
            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MaxValue, int.MaxValue);
            Assert.Equal(-2, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddTwoMinIntegersHandlesOverflow()
        {
            // If Add is called with int.MinValue and int.MinValue, then the method must handle the overflow condition consistent with C# default integer arithmetic. Thus, it should return 0 (since -2147483648 + -2147483648 wraps around).
            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MinValue, int.MinValue);
            Assert.Equal(0, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddMaxIntAndZeroReturnsMaxInt()
        {
            // If Add is called with int.MaxValue and 0, then the method should return int.MaxValue. This verifies that adding zero to a boundary value does not alter it.
            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MaxValue, 0);
            Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddMinIntAndZeroReturnsMinInt()
        {
            // If Add is called with int.MinValue and 0, then the method should return int.MinValue. This verifies that adding zero to a boundary value does not alter it.
            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MinValue, 0);
            Assert.Equal(int.MinValue, result);
        }

        [Fact]
        public void TestAddCommutativePropertyHolds()
        {
            // If Add(a, b) is called and Add(b, a) is called with arbitrary integers (e.g., 5 and 10), then both calls should return the same result. This verifies the commutative property of addition, which is fundamental to the concept of 'adding'.
            var result1 = Calculator.Add(5, 10);
            var result2 = Calculator.Add(10, 5);
            Assert.Equal(result1, result2);
        }
    }
}

     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

using Xunit;
using Calc;

namespace Calc.Tests
{

public class CalculatorTest
{
    [Fact]
    public void TestSubTrivialCaseZeroFromZero()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting zero from zero results in zero. This tests the base case of the operation where both operands are the additive identity.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(0, 0);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubPositiveIntegersResultPositive()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a smaller positive integer from a larger positive integer yields the correct positive difference (e.g., 10 - 3 = 7). This confirms standard subtraction logic for positive operands.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(10, 3);
        Assert.Equal(7, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubPositiveIntegersResultNegative()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a larger positive integer from a smaller positive integer yields the correct negative difference (e.g., 3 - 10 = -7). This confirms the method correctly handles sign inversion when the subtrahend is greater than the minuend.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(3, 10);
        Assert.Equal(-7, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubEqualPositiveIntegersResultZero()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a positive integer from an equal positive integer results in zero (e.g., 5 - 5 = 0). This tests the cancellation property of subtraction.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(5, 5);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubNegativeIntegerFromPositive()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative integer from a positive integer is equivalent to addition (e.g., 5 - (-3) = 8). This ensures the method correctly handles the double negative logic inherent in integer arithmetic.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(5, -3);
        Assert.Equal(8, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubPositiveIntegerFromNegative()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a positive integer from a negative integer results in a more negative number (e.g., -5 - 3 = -8). This tests correct magnitude increase in the negative direction.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(-5, 3);
        Assert.Equal(-8, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubNegativeIntegerFromNegative()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative integer from a negative integer results in a value closer to zero or positive (e.g., -5 - (-3) = -2). This confirms correct handling of signs when both operands are negative.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(-5, -3);
        Assert.Equal(-2, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubZeroFromPositive()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting zero from a positive integer returns the original positive integer (e.g., 10 - 0 = 10). This tests the identity property where the subtrahend is zero.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(10, 0);
        Assert.Equal(10, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubZeroFromNegative()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting zero from a negative integer returns the original negative integer (e.g., -10 - 0 = -10). This confirms the identity property holds for negative operands.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(-10, 0);
        Assert.Equal(-10, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubPositiveFromZero()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a positive integer from zero yields the negative of that integer (e.g., 0 - 5 = -5). This tests the sign inversion property when the minuend is zero.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(0, 5);
        Assert.Equal(-5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubNegativeFromZero()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting a negative integer from zero yields the positive absolute value of that integer (e.g., 0 - (-5) = 5). This tests the conversion of negative subtrahends to positive results when starting from zero.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(0, -5);
        Assert.Equal(5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubMaxValueMinusOne()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting 1 from int.MaxValue results in int.MaxValue - 1 (2147483646). This tests the upper boundary of the integer range without overflow.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(int.MaxValue, 1);
        Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue - 1, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubMinValueMinusOneOverflowBehavior()
    {
        // Verify the behavior when subtracting 1 from int.MinValue. Since int.MinValue - 1 exceeds the minimum representable integer, this test checks if the implementation throws an OverflowException (if checked) or wraps around (if unchecked). Documentation does not specify, so we verify consistent arithmetic behavior.
        // Standard C# unchecked arithmetic wraps around.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(int.MinValue, 1);
        Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubMaxValueMinusMaxValue()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting int.MaxValue from int.MaxValue results in 0. This tests the cancellation property at the upper boundary.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(int.MaxValue, int.MaxValue);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubMinValueMinusMinValue()
    {
        // Verify that subtracting int.MinValue from int.MinValue results in 0. This tests the cancellation property at the lower boundary.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(int.MinValue, int.MinValue);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubMaxValueMinusMinValueOverflowBehavior()
    {
        // Verify the behavior when subtracting int.MinValue from int.MaxValue. The mathematical result is 4,294,967,295, which exceeds int.MaxValue. This test checks for OverflowException or wrap-around behavior.
        // Standard C# unchecked arithmetic wraps around.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(int.MaxValue, int.MinValue);
        Assert.Equal(-1, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubMinValueMinusMaxValueOverflowBehavior()
    {
        // Verify the behavior when subtracting int.MaxValue from int.MinValue. The mathematical result is -4,294,967,295, which is less than int.MinValue. This test checks for OverflowException or wrap-around behavior.
        // Standard C# unchecked arithmetic wraps around.
        int result = Calculator.Sub(int.MinValue, int.MaxValue);
        Assert.Equal(1, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestSubNonCommutativeCheck()
    {
        // Verify that Sub(a, b) is not equal to Sub(b, a) for distinct a and b (e.g., Sub(10, 3) != Sub(3, 10)). This confirms the method follows standard subtraction logic which is non-commutative.
        int result1 = Calculator.Sub(10, 3);
        int result2 = Calculator.Sub(3, 10);
        Assert.NotEqual(result1, result2);
    }
}

     1	namespace Calc;
     2	
     3	/// <summary>
     4	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

using Xunit;
using Calc;

namespace Calc.Tests
{

public class CalculatorTest
{
    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnPositiveProductForTwoPositiveIntegers()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with two positive integers (e.g., 5 and 4), then it should return their correct mathematical product (20). This tests the basic functionality described in 'Multiplies two integers' for standard positive cases.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(5, 4);
        Assert.Equal(20, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnNegativeProductForNegativeAndPositiveInteger()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with a negative integer and a positive integer (e.g., -5 and 4), then it should return a negative integer equal to their mathematical product (-20). This verifies correct sign handling for mixed operands.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(-5, 4);
        Assert.Equal(-20, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnNegativeProductForPositiveAndNegativeInteger()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with a positive integer and a negative integer (e.g., 5 and -4), then it should return a negative integer equal to their mathematical product (-20). This verifies correct sign handling when the second operand is negative.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(5, -4);
        Assert.Equal(-20, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnPositiveProductForTwoNegativeIntegers()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with two negative integers (e.g., -5 and -4), then it should return a positive integer equal to their mathematical product (20). This verifies that the method correctly handles the rule that a negative times a negative equals a positive.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(-5, -4);
        Assert.Equal(20, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnZeroWhenFirstOperandIsZero()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with zero as the first argument and any non-zero integer as the second argument (e.g., 0 and 5), then it should return 0. This tests the multiplicative property of zero.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(0, 5);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnZeroWhenSecondOperandIsZero()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with any non-zero integer as the first argument and zero as the second argument (e.g., 5 and 0), then it should return 0. This tests the multiplicative property of zero when zero is the second operand.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(5, 0);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnZeroWhenBothOperandsAreZero()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with zero for both arguments (e.g., 0 and 0), then it should return 0. This tests the edge case of zero multiplied by zero.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(0, 0);
        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnSameValueWhenMultipliedByOne()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with any integer as the first argument and 1 as the second argument (e.g., 5 and 1), then it should return the value of the first argument (5). This tests the multiplicative identity property.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(5, 1);
        Assert.Equal(5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnSameValueWhenOneIsFirstOperand()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with 1 as the first argument and any integer as the second argument (e.g., 1 and 5), then it should return the value of the second argument (5). This tests the multiplicative identity property when 1 is the first operand.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(1, 5);
        Assert.Equal(5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnNegatedValueWhenMultipliedByNegativeOne()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with any integer and -1 (e.g., 5 and -1), then it should return the additive inverse (negation) of that integer (-5). This tests behavior with the additive inverse identity.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(5, -1);
        Assert.Equal(-5, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnMinValueWhenMultiplyingMinValueByOne()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with int.MinValue and 1, then it should return int.MinValue. This tests the boundary condition where the result is exactly the minimum integer value.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(int.MinValue, 1);
        Assert.Equal(int.MinValue, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnMaxValueWhenMultiplyingMaxValueByOne()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with int.MaxValue and 1, then it should return int.MaxValue. This tests the boundary condition where the result is exactly the maximum integer value.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(int.MaxValue, 1);
        Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldHandleOverflowWithStandardCSharpBehavior()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with two integers whose mathematical product exceeds the range of int (e.g., int.MaxValue and 2), then it should return the wrapped/low-order 32 bits result as per default C# unchecked arithmetic behavior, OR throw OverflowException if executed in a checked context. This tests implicit overflow handling since no specific exception behavior is documented.
        // In default unchecked context, int.MaxValue * 2 overflows.
        // Expected result: (int.MaxValue * 2) in unchecked context
        // int.MaxValue is 2147483647. 2147483647 * 2 = 4294967294.
        // 4294967294 in hex is 0xFFFFFFFЕ.
        // In 32-bit signed, this is -2.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(int.MaxValue, 2);
        Assert.Equal(-2, result);
    }

    [Fact]
    public void TestMulShouldReturnCorrectProductForLargeNumbersWithinRange()
    {
        // If the method Mul is called with two large integers whose product fits within the int range (e.g., 100000 and 100000 is too big, but 40000 and 5000 = 200,000,000), then it should return the exact correct mathematical product (200,000,000). This tests correctness for larger but valid inputs.
        var result = Calculator.Mul(40000, 5000);
        Assert.Equal(200000000, result);
    }
}

     6	public class Calculator

     8	    /// <summary>

     2	/// <summary>
     3	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     4	/// </summary>
     5	public class Calculator
     6	{
     7	    /// <summary>
     8	    /// Adds two integers.
     9	    /// </summary>
    10	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    11	    {
    12	        return a + b;
    13	    }
    14	
    15	    /// <summary>
    16	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    17	    /// </summary>
    18	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    19	    {
    20	        return a - b;
    21	    }
    22	
    23	    /// <summary>
    24	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    25	    /// </summary>
    26	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    27	    {
    28	        return a * b;
    29	    }

This tests implicit overflow handling since no specific exception behavior is documented.
   110	        // In default unchecked context, int.MaxValue * 2 overflows.
   111	        // Expected result: (int.MaxValue * 2) in unchecked context
   112	        // int.MaxValue is 2147483647. 2147483647 * 2 = 4294967294.
   113	        // 4294967294 in hex is 0xFFFFFFFЕ.
   114	        // In 32-bit signed, this is -2.

     3	/// <summary>
     4	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

     2	using Xunit;
     3	using Calc;
     4	
     5	namespace Calc.Tests
     6	{
     7	    public class CalculatorTest
     8	    {
     9	        [Fact]
    10	        public void TestAddTwoPositiveIntegersReturnsSum()
    11	        {
    12	            // If Add is called with two positive integers (e.g., 5 and 10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (15). This verifies the basic functionality described in the documentation for standard positive inputs.
    13	            var result = Calculator.Add(5, 10);
    14	            Assert.Equal(15, result);
    15	        }
    16	
    17	        [Fact]
    18	        public void TestAddTwoNegativeIntegersReturnsSum()
    19	        {
    20	            // If Add is called with two negative integers (e.g., -5 and -10), then the method should return their arithmetic sum (-15). This verifies that the addition logic correctly handles negative numbers as implied by 'Adds two integers'.
    21	            var result = Calculator.Add(-5, -10);
    22	            Assert.Equal(-15, result);
    23	        }
    24	
    25	        [Fact]
    26	        public void TestAddPositiveAndNegativeIntegersReturnsSum()
    27	        {
    28	            // If Add is called with a positive integer and a negative integer (e.g., 10 and -5), then the method should return the correct net result (5). This tests the handling of mixed signs.
    29	            var result = Calculator.Add(10, -5);
    30	            Assert.Equal(5, result);
    31	        }
    32	
    33	        [Fact]
    34	        public void TestAddOppositeNumbersReturnsZero()
    35	        {
    36	            // If Add is called with two integers that are additive inverses (e.g., 5 and -5), then the method should return 0. This verifies the identity property of addition for non-zero inputs.
    37	            var result = Calculator.Add(5, -5);
    38	            Assert.Equal(0, result);
    39	        }
    40	
    41	        [Fact]
    42	        public void TestAddZeroToPositiveIntegerReturnsOriginal()
    43	        {
    44	            // If Add is called with a positive integer and zero (e.g., 10 and 0), then the method should return the original positive integer (10). This verifies that zero acts as the additive identity.
    45	            var result = Calculator.Add(10, 0);
    46	            Assert.Equal(10, result);
    47	        }
    48	
    49	        [Fact]
    50	        public void TestAddZeroToNegativeIntegerReturnsOriginal()
    51	        {
    52	            // If Add is called with a negative integer and zero (e.g., -10 and 0), then the method should return the original negative integer (-10). This verifies that zero acts as the additive identity for negative numbers.
    53	            var result = Calculator.Add(-10, 0);
    54	            Assert.Equal(-10, result);
    55	        }
    56	
    57	        [Fact]
    58	        public void TestAddZeroToZeroReturnsZero()
    59	        {
    60	            // If Add is called with zero and zero (0 and 0), then the method should return 0. This is the most basic case of the additive identity.
    61	            var result = Calculator.Add(0, 0);
    62	            Assert.Equal(0, result);
    63	        }
    64	
    65	        [Fact]
    66	        public void TestAddMaxIntAndMinIntReturnsMinusOne()
    67	        {
    68	            // If Add is called with int.MaxValue and int.MinValue, then the method should return -1. This tests the boundary behavior where the sum does not overflow the 32-bit integer range but hits the lower boundary limit.
    69	            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MaxValue, int.MinValue);
    70	            Assert.Equal(-1, result);
    71	        }
    72	
    73	        [Fact]
    74	        public void TestAddTwoMaxIntegersHandlesOverflow()
    75	        {
    76	            // If Add is called with int.MaxValue and int.MaxValue, then the method must handle the overflow condition consistent with C# default integer arithmetic (unchecked wrap-around to int.MinValue + 2, i.e., -2) or throw OverflowException if checked. Since documentation does not specify exception handling, we verify the actual implementation behavior against the default CLR behavior for 'int' addition, which is wrap-around. Thus, it should return -2.
    77	            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MaxValue, int.MaxValue);
    78	            Assert.Equal(-2, result);
    79	        }
    80	
    81	        [Fact]
    82	        public void TestAddTwoMinIntegersHandlesOverflow()
    83	        {
    84	            // If Add is called with int.MinValue and int.MinValue, then the method must handle the overflow condition consistent with C# default integer arithmetic. Thus, it should return 0 (since -2147483648 + -2147483648 wraps around).
    85	            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MinValue, int.MinValue);
    86	            Assert.Equal(0, result);
    87	        }
    88	
    89	        [Fact]
    90	        public void TestAddMaxIntAndZeroReturnsMaxInt()
    91	        {

    93	            var result = Calculator.Add(int.MaxValue, 0);
    94	            Assert.Equal(int.MaxValue, result);
    95	        }

     1	namespace Calc;
     2	
     3	/// <summary>
     4	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

    15	        Assert.Equal(0, result);
    16	    }

    22	        int result = Calculator.Sub(10, 3);
    23	        Assert.Equal(7, result);
    24	    }
    25

    30	        int result = Calculator.Sub(3, 10);
    31	        Assert.Equal(-7, result);
    32	    }

     1	namespace Calc;
     2	
     3	/// <summary>
     4	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     5	/// </summary>
     6	public class Calculator
     7	{
     8	    /// <summary>
     9	    /// Adds two integers.
    10	    /// </summary>
    11	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    12	    {
    13	        return a + b;
    14	    }
    15	
    16	    /// <summary>
    17	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    18	    /// </summary>
    19	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    20	    {
    21	        return a - b;
    22	    }
    23	
    24	    /// <summary>
    25	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    26	    /// </summary>
    27	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    28	    {
    29	        return a / b;
    30	    }
    31	}

    12	    {
    13	        // If the method Mul is called with two positive integers (e.g., 5 and 4), then it should return their correct mathematical product (20). This tests the basic functionality described in 'Multiplies two integers' for standard positive cases.
    14	        var result = Calculator.Mul(5, 4);
    15	        Assert.Equal(20, result);
    16	    }
    17	
    18	    [Fact]

    20	    {
    21	        // If the method Mul is called with a negative integer and a positive integer (e.g., -5 and 4), then it should return a negative integer equal to their mathematical product (-20). This verifies correct sign handling for mixed operands.

    23	        Assert.Equal(-20, result);
    24	    }

   107	    public void TestMulShouldHandleOverflowWithStandardCSharpBehavior()
   108	    {
   109	        // If the method Mul is called with two integers whose mathematical product exceeds the range of int (e.g., int.MaxValue and 2), then it should return the wrapped/low-order 32 bits result as per default C# unchecked arithmetic behavior, OR throw OverflowException if executed in a checked context. This tests implicit overflow handling since no specific exception behavior is documented.
   110	        // In default unchecked context, int.MaxValue * 2 overflows.
   111	        // Expected result: (int.MaxValue * 2) in unchecked context
   112	        // int.MaxValue is 2147483647. 2147483647 * 2 = 4294967294.
   113	        // 4294967294 in hex is 0xFFFFFFFЕ.
   114	        // In 32-bit signed, this is -2.
   115	        var result = Calculator.Mul(int.MaxValue, 2);
   116	        Assert.Equal(-2, result);
   117	    }
   118	
   119	    [Fact]
   120	    public void TestMulShouldReturnCorrectProductForLargeNumbersWithinRange()
   121	    {
   122	        // If the method Mul is called with two large integers whose product fits within the int range (e.g., 100000 and 100000 is too big, but 40000 and 5000 = 200,000,000), then it should return the exact correct mathematical product (200,000,000). This tests correctness for larger but valid inputs.
   123	        var result = Calculator.Mul(40000, 5000);
   124	        Assert.Equal(200000000, result);
   125	    }

     1	namespace Calc;
     2	/// <summary>
     3	/// Provides basic calculator operations.
     4	/// </summary>
     5	public class Calculator
     6	{
     7	    /// <summary>
     8	    /// Adds two integers.
     9	    /// </summary>
    10	    public static int Add(int a, int b)
    11	    {
    12	        return a + b;
    13	    }
    14	
    15	    /// <summary>
    16	    /// Subtracts the second integer from the first integer.
    17	    /// </summary>
    18	    public static int Sub(int a, int b)
    19	    {
    20	        return a - b;
    21	    }
    22	
    23	    /// <summary>
    24	    /// Multiplies two integers.
    25	    /// </summary>
    26	    public static int Mul(int a, int b)
    27	    {
    28	        return a * b;
    29	    }
    30	}     1

   108	    {
   109	        // If the method Mul is called with two integers whose mathematical product exceeds the range of int (e.g., int.MaxValue and 2), then it should return the wrapped/low-order 32 bits result as per default C# unchecked arithmetic behavior, OR throw OverflowException if executed in a checked context. This tests implicit overflow handling since no specific exception behavior is documented.
   110	        // In default unchecked context, int.MaxValue * 2 overflows.
   111	        // Expected result: (int.MaxValue * 2) in unchecked context
   112	        // int.MaxValue is 2147483647. 2147483647 * 2 = 4294967294.
   113	        // 4294967294 in hex is 0xFFFFFFFЕ.
   114	        // In 32-bit signed, this is -2.
   115	        var result = Calculator.Mul(int.MaxValue, 2);
   116	        Assert.Equal(-2, result);
   117	    }

   123	        var result = Calculator.Mul(40000, 5000);
